**Asia** ([/ˈeɪʒə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/2/2e/En-us-Asia.ogg/En-us-Asia.ogg.mp3 "Play audio")[ⓘ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:En-us-Asia.ogg "File:En-us-Asia.ogg") [*AY-zhə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key "Help:Pronunciation respelling key"), [UK](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English "British English") also [/ˈeɪʃə/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:IPA/English "Help:IPA/English") [*AY-shə*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key "Help:Pronunciation respelling key")) is the largest [continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent "Continent")[\[note 1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10)[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11)[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Boudreau-12) in the world by both land area and population.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Boudreau-12) It covers an area of more than 44 million square kilometres,[\[note 2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-13) about 30% of Earth's total land area and 8% of Earth's total surface area. The continent, which has long been home to the majority of the [human population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_population "Human population"),[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-14) was the site of many of the [first civilisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_civilisations "First civilisations"). Its 4.7 billion people constitute roughly 60% of the world's population.

Asia shares the [landmass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmass "Landmass") of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia "Eurasia") with [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europe "Europe"), and of [Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia "Afro-Eurasia") with both Europe and [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa "Africa"). In general terms, it is bounded on the east by the [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean"), on the south by the [Indian Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Ocean "Indian Ocean"), and on the north by the [Arctic Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Ocean "Arctic Ocean"). The border of Asia with Europe is a [historical and cultural construct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_constructionism "Social constructionism"), as there is no clear physical and geographical separation between them. A commonly accepted division places Asia to the east of the [Suez Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Canal "Suez Canal") separating it from Africa; and to the east of the [Turkish straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_straits "Turkish straits"), the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains "Ural Mountains") and [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River "Ural River"), and to the south of the [Caucasus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains "Caucasus Mountains") and the [Caspian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_Sea "Caspian Sea") and [Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea "Black Sea") seas, separating it from Europe.[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-National_Geographic-1999-15)

Since the concept of Asia derives from the term for the eastern region from a European perspective, Asia is the remaining vast area of Eurasia minus Europe. Therefore, Asia is a region where various independent cultures coexist rather than sharing a single culture, and the boundary between Europe is somewhat arbitrary and has moved since its first conception in [classical antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_antiquity "Classical antiquity"). The division of Eurasia into two continents reflects [East–West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_dichotomy "East–West dichotomy") cultural differences, some of which vary on a spectrum.

[China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India") traded places as the [largest economies in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_regions_by_past_GDP_\(PPP\) "List of regions by past GDP (PPP)") from 1 to 1800 CE. China was a major economic power for much of recorded history, with the highest [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita "GDP per capita") until 1500.[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-16)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Eric.ed.gov-2000-17)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) The [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road "Silk Road") became the main east–west trading route in the Asian hinterlands while the [Straits of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straits_of_Malacca "Straits of Malacca") stood as a major sea route. Asia has exhibited [economic dynamism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_dynamism "Economic dynamism") as well as robust population growth during the 20th century, but overall population growth has since fallen.[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19) Asia was the birthplace of most of the world's mainstream religions including [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism"), [Zoroastrianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoroastrianism "Zoroastrianism"), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism "Judaism"), [Jainism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jainism "Jainism"), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism"), [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism "Confucianism"), [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism "Taoism"), [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity"), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam "Islam"), [Sikhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sikhism "Sikhism"), and many other religions.

Asia varies greatly across and within [its regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Asia "Regions of Asia") with regard to ethnic groups, cultures, environments, economics, historical ties, and government systems. It also has a mix of many different climates ranging from the equatorial south via the hot deserts in parts of [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia "West Asia"), [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia") and [South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia "South Asia"), temperate areas in the east and the continental centre to vast subarctic and polar areas in [North Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Asia "North Asia").

## Definition and boundaries

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Definition and boundaries")]

Further information on Asian borders: [Geography of Asia § Boundary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Asia#Boundary "Geography of Asia"), [Boundaries between the continents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundaries_between_the_continents "Boundaries between the continents"), [List of transcontinental countries § Asia and Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_transcontinental_countries#Asia_and_Europe "List of transcontinental countries"), and [Copenhagen criteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copenhagen_criteria "Copenhagen criteria")

### Asia–Africa boundary

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Asia–Africa boundary")]

The boundary between Asia and Africa is the [Suez Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Canal "Suez Canal"), the [Gulf of Suez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Suez "Gulf of Suez"), the [Red Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea "Red Sea"), and the [Bab-el-Mandeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bab-el-Mandeb "Bab-el-Mandeb").[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20) This makes [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt "Egypt") a [transcontinental country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_country "Transcontinental country"), with the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula "Sinai Peninsula") in Asia and the remainder of the country in Africa.

### Asia–Europe boundary

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Asia–Europe boundary")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/73/Possible_definitions_of_the_boundary_between_Europe_and_Asia.png/260px-Possible_definitions_of_the_boundary_between_Europe_and_Asia.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Possible_definitions_of_the_boundary_between_Europe_and_Asia.png)

Definitions used for the boundary between Asia and Europe in different periods of history. [Modern definitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#Ongoing_definition) mostly fit with lines *B* and *F* given.

The threefold division of the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World "Old World") into Africa, Asia, and Europe has been in use since the 6th century BCE, due to [Greek geographers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_geographers "Greek geographers") such as [Anaximander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaximander "Anaximander") and [Hecataeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hecataeus_of_Miletus "Hecataeus of Miletus").[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-21) Anaximander placed the boundary between Asia and Europe along the [Phasis River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phasis_River "Phasis River") (now the Rioni) in [Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_\(country\) "Georgia (country)") of Caucasus (from its mouth by [Poti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poti "Poti") on the [Black Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea "Black Sea") coast, through the [Surami Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surami_Pass "Surami Pass") and along the [Kura River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kura_\(Caspian_Sea\) "Kura (Caspian Sea)") to the Caspian Sea), a convention still followed by [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus "Herodotus") in the 5th century BCE.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22) During the [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period "Hellenistic period"),[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-23) this convention was revised, and the boundary between Europe and Asia was now considered to be the [Tanais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_River_\(Russia\) "Don River (Russia)") (the modern Don River). This is the convention used by Roman era authors such as [Posidonius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posidonius "Posidonius"),[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24) [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo "Strabo")[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25) and [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy "Ptolemy").[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-26)

The border between Asia and Europe was historically defined by European academics.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Lineback-2013-27)

In Sweden, five years after Peter's death, in 1730 [Philip Johan von Strahlenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_Johan_von_Strahlenberg "Philip Johan von Strahlenberg") published a new atlas proposing the Ural Mountains as the border of Asia. Tatishchev announced that he had proposed the idea to von Strahlenberg. The latter had suggested the [Emba River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emba_River "Emba River") as the lower boundary. Over the next century various proposals were made until the [Ural River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_River "Ural River") prevailed in the mid-19th century. The border had been moved perforce from the Black Sea to the Caspian Sea into which the Ural River projects.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-28) The border between the Black Sea and the Caspian is usually placed along the crest of the [Caucasus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains "Caucasus Mountains"), although it is sometimes placed further north.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Lineback-2013-27)

### Asia–Oceania boundary

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Asia–Oceania boundary")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg/260px-Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Map_of_Sunda_and_Sahul.svg)

Definitions of the boundary between Asia and Oceania

The border between Asia and [Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceania "Oceania") is usually placed somewhere in the [Indonesian Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indonesian_Archipelago "Indonesian Archipelago"), specifically in [Eastern Indonesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Indonesia "Eastern Indonesia"). The [Wallace Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallace_Line "Wallace Line") separates the Asian and [Wallacea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallacea "Wallacea") biogeographical realms, a transition zone of deep water straits between the Asian and Australian continental shelves. [Weber's Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weber%27s_Line "Weber's Line") split the region in two with regard to the balance of fauna between Asian origin or Australo-Papuan origin.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Simpson-1977-29) Wallacea's eastern boundary with [Sahul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_\(continent\) "Australia (continent)") is represented by the [Lydekker's Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Lydekker "Richard Lydekker").[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30)[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31) The [Maluku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands "Maluku Islands") (except the Aru Islands) are often considered to lie on the border of southeast Asia, with the [Aru Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aru_Islands "Aru Islands") and [Western New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_New_Guinea "Western New Guinea"), to the east of the Lydekker's Line, being wholly part of Oceania, as both lie on the Australian continental plate.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Simpson-1977-29) Culturally, the Wallacea region denoted the transition between [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_people "Austronesian people") and [Melanesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesian_people "Melanesian people"), with varying degrees of intermixing between the two. In general, the further west and coastal a region is, the stronger the Austronesian influences, and the further east and inland a region is, the stronger the Melanesian influences.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) The terms Southeast Asia and Oceania, devised in the 19th century, have had several vastly different geographic meanings since their inception. The chief factor in determining which islands of the Indonesian Archipelago are Asian has been the location of the colonial possessions of the various empires there (not all European). Lewis and Wigen assert, "The narrowing of 'Southeast Asia' to its present boundaries was thus a gradual process."[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Us-su-maritime.jpg/260px-Us-su-maritime.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Us-su-maritime.jpg)

The United States–Russia borrder according to the [USSR–USA Maritime Boundary Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USSR%E2%80%93USA_Maritime_Boundary_Agreement "USSR–USA Maritime Boundary Agreement")

The [Bering Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Strait "Bering Strait") and [Bering Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Sea "Bering Sea") separate the landmasses of Asia and [North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_America "North America"), as well as forming the international boundary between Russia and the United States. This [national](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_boundary "National boundary") and continental boundary separates the [Diomede Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diomede_Islands "Diomede Islands") in the Bering Strait, with [Big Diomede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diomede_Islands "Diomede Islands") in [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia "Russia") and [Little Diomede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diomede_Islands "Diomede Islands") in the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States"). The [Aleutian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleutian_Islands "Aleutian Islands") are an island chain extending westward from the [Alaskan Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaskan_Peninsula "Alaskan Peninsula") toward Russia's [Komandorski Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komandorski_Islands "Komandorski Islands") and [Kamchatka Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamchatka_Peninsula "Kamchatka Peninsula"). Most of them are always associated with North America, except for the westernmost [Near Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_Islands "Near Islands") group, which is on Asia's continental shelf beyond the [North Aleutians Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Aleutians_Basin "North Aleutians Basin") and on rare occasions could be associated with Asia, which could then allow the United States to be considered a transcontinental state. The Aleutian Islands are sometimes associated with Oceania, owing to their status as remote Pacific islands, and their proximity to the Pacific Plate.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-34)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35)[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-36) This is extremely rare however, due to their non-tropical biogeography, as well as their [inhabitants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleut "Aleut"), who have historically been related to the [Indigenous peoples of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas "Indigenous peoples of the Americas").[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37)[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38)

[St. Lawrence Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Lawrence_Island "St. Lawrence Island") in the northern Bering Sea belongs to the US state of [Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaska "Alaska") and may be associated with either continent but is almost always considered part of North America, as with the [Rat Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rat_Islands "Rat Islands") in the Aleutian chain. At their nearest points, Alaska and Russia are separated by only 4 kilometres (2.5 miles).

### Ongoing definition

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Ongoing definition")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg/220px-Afro-Eurasia_%28orthographic_projection%29_political.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Afro-Eurasia_\(orthographic_projection\)_political.svg)

Afro-Eurasia

Geographical Asia is a cultural artifact of European conceptions of the world, beginning with the [Ancient Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greeks "Ancient Greeks"), being imposed onto other cultures, an imprecise concept causing endemic contention about what it means. Asia does not exactly correspond to the cultural borders of its various types of constituents.[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39)

From the time of Herodotus, a minority of geographers have rejected the three-continent system (Europe, Africa, Asia) on the grounds that there is no substantial physical separation between them.[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-40) For example, Sir [Barry Cunliffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Cunliffe "Barry Cunliffe"), the emeritus professor of European archeology at Oxford, argues that Europe has been geographically and culturally merely "the western excrescence of the continent of Asia".[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-41)

Geographically, Asia is the major eastern constituent of the continent of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia "Eurasia") with Europe being a northwestern [peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peninsula "Peninsula") of the landmass. Asia, Europe and Africa make up a single continuous landmass—[Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia "Afro-Eurasia")—and share a common [continental shelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelf "Continental shelf"). Almost all of Europe and a major part of Asia sit atop the [Eurasian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Plate "Eurasian Plate"), adjoined on the south by the [Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate "Arabian Plate") and [Indian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_Plate "Indian Plate") and with the easternmost part of Siberia (east of the [Chersky Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chersky_Range "Chersky Range")) on the [North American Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Plate "North American Plate").

## Etymology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Etymology")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f6/Gulf5..JPG/290px-Gulf5..JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gulf5..JPG)

Ptolemy's Asia

The term "Asia" is believed to originate in the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age "Bronze Age") toponym *[Assuwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assuwa "Assuwa")* ([Hittite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittite_language "Hittite language"): 𒀸𒋗𒉿, romanized: *aš-šu-wa*) which originally referred only to a portion of northwestern [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia "Anatolia"). The term appears in [Hittite records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittite_texts "Hittite texts") recounting how a confederation of Assuwan states including [Troy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troy "Troy") unsuccessfully rebelled against the Hittite king [Tudhaliya I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudhaliya_I "Tudhaliya I") around 1400 BCE.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-42)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) Roughly contemporary [Linear B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linear_B "Linear B") documents contain the term *aswia* ([Mycenaean Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mycenaean_Greek_language "Mycenaean Greek language"): 𐀀𐀯𐀹𐀊, romanized: *a-si-wi-ja*), seemingly in reference to captives from the same area.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45)[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ca/Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg/260px-Roman_Empire_-_Asia_%28125_AD%29.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Roman_Empire_-_Asia_\(125_AD\).svg)

The province of [Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_\(Roman_province\) "Asia (Roman province)") within the Roman Empire

Herodotus used the term in reference to [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia "Anatolia") and the territory of the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire "Achaemenid Empire"), in contrast to Greece and Egypt. He reports that Greeks assumed that Asia was named after the wife of [Prometheus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prometheus "Prometheus"), but that [Lydians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia "Lydia") say it was named after *Asies*, son of Cotys, who passed the name on to a tribe at [Sardis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sardis "Sardis").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47) In [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology "Greek mythology"), "Asia" (Ἀσία or Ἀσίη) was the name of a "[Nymph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymph "Nymph") or [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(mythology\) "Titan (mythology)") goddess of Lydia".[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-48) The [Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad "Iliad") (attributed by the ancient Greeks to [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer")) mentions two Phrygians in the [Trojan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_War "Trojan War") named [Asios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asius_\(mythology\) "Asius (mythology)") (literally 'Asian');[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49) and also a marsh or lowland containing a marsh in Lydia as ασιος.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50)

The term was later adopted by the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_people "Roman people"), who used it in reference to the [province of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Province_of_Asia "Province of Asia"), located in western Anatolia.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-51) One of the first writers to use Asia as a name of the whole continent was [Pliny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder "Pliny the Elder").[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-52)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: History")]

Main article: [History of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Asia "History of Asia")

### Ancient era

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Ancient era")]

See also: [Sinosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinosphere "Sinosphere"), [Greater India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_India "Greater India"), and [Greater Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Iran "Greater Iran")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Silkroutes.jpg/260px-Silkroutes.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Silkroutes.jpg)

The [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road "Silk Road") connected civilisations across Asia[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-53)

The history of Asia can be seen as the distinct histories of several peripheral coastal regions: [East Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia "East Asia"), [South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia "South Asia"), [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia"), [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia"), and [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia "West Asia"). The coastal periphery was home to some of the world's earliest known civilisations, each of them developing around fertile river valleys. The civilisations in [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia "Mesopotamia"), the [Indus Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley "Indus Valley") and the [Yellow River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_River "Yellow River") shared many similarities. These civilisations may well have exchanged technologies and ideas such as [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics "Mathematics") and the [wheel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wheel "Wheel"). Other innovations, such as writing, seem to have been developed individually in each area. Cities, states and empires developed in these lowlands.

The central steppe region had long been inhabited by horse-mounted nomads who could reach all areas of Asia from the [steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe "Steppe"). The earliest postulated expansion out of the steppe is that of the [Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Europeans "Indo-Europeans"), who spread their languages into West Asia, South Asia, and the borders of China, where the [Tocharians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tocharians "Tocharians") resided. The northernmost part of Asia, including much of [Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberia "Siberia"), was largely inaccessible to the steppe nomads, owing to the dense forests, climate and [tundra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tundra "Tundra"). These areas remained very sparsely populated.

The center and the peripheries were mostly kept separated by mountains and deserts. The [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus "Caucasus") and [Himalaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalaya "Himalaya") mountains and the [Karakum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karakum_Desert "Karakum Desert") and [Gobi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobi "Gobi") deserts formed barriers that the steppe horsemen could cross only with difficulty. While the urban city dwellers were more advanced technologically and socially, in many cases they could do little in a military aspect to defend against the mounted hordes of the steppe. However, the lowlands did not have enough open grasslands to support a large equestrian force; for this and other reasons, the nomads who conquered states in China, India, and the Middle East often found themselves adapting to the local, more affluent societies.

### Medieval era

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Medieval era")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3f/Mongol_dominions1.jpg/260px-Mongol_dominions1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mongol_dominions1.jpg)

The [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire "Mongol Empire") at its greatest extent. The gray area is the later [Timurid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Empire "Timurid Empire")

The Islamic [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate "Caliphate")'s defeats of the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine "Byzantine") and Persian empires led to West Asia and southern parts of [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia") and [western parts of South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_South_Asia "Northwestern South Asia") under its control during [its conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests "Early Muslim conquests") of the 7th century; Islam also [spread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam "Spread of Islam") over centuries to the southern regions of India and Southeast Asia through trade along the [Maritime Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road "Maritime Silk Road").[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-54)[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-55) The [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire "Mongol Empire") conquered a large part of Asia in the 13th century, an area extending from China to Europe. Before the Mongol invasion, [Song dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_dynasty "Song dynasty") reportedly had approximately 120 million citizens; the 1300 census which followed the invasion reported roughly 60 million people.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56)

The [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death "Black Death"), one of the most devastating [pandemics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandemic "Pandemic") in human history, is thought to have originated in the arid plains of central Asia, where it then travelled along the [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road "Silk Road").[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-57)

### Modern era

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Modern era")]

See also: [History of Eurasia § Modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Eurasia#Modern_era "History of Eurasia"), and [Afro-Asia § Modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Asia#Modern_era "Afro-Asia")

European involvement in Asia became more significant from the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery "Age of Discovery") onward, with Iberian-sponsored sailors such as [Christopher Columbus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christopher_Columbus "Christopher Columbus") and [Vasco da Gama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama "Vasco da Gama") paving the way for new routes from [Atlantic Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Europe "Atlantic Europe") to [Pacific Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Asia "Pacific Asia") and the Indian Ocean respectively in the late 15th century.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58) The [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire "Russian Empire") also began to expand into northwestern Asia from the 17th century, and would eventually take control of all of Siberia and most of Central Asia by the end of the 19th century.

Among non-European empires, the [Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Empire "Ottoman Empire") controlled Anatolia, most of the Middle East, North Africa and the Balkans from the mid 16th century onward, while in the 17th century, the [Manchu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manchu "Manchu") conquered China and established the [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty "Qing dynasty"). The Islamic [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire "Mughal Empire") (preceded by the [Delhi Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delhi_Sultanate "Delhi Sultanate") of the 13th to early 16th century)[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-59) and the Hindu [Maratha Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maratha_Empire "Maratha Empire") controlled much of India in the 16th and 18th centuries respectively.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Robert_Clive_and_Mir_Jafar_after_the_Battle_of_Plassey%2C_1757_by_Francis_Hayman.jpg/120px-Robert_Clive_and_Mir_Jafar_after_the_Battle_of_Plassey%2C_1757_by_Francis_Hayman.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Robert_Clive_and_Mir_Jafar_after_the_Battle_of_Plassey,_1757_by_Francis_Hayman.jpg)

The aftermath of the 1757 [Battle of Plassey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey "Battle of Plassey"), which eventually led to British India.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/China%2C_the_cake_of_kings_and_emperors%2C_Le_Petit_Journal_1898.jpg/65px-China%2C_the_cake_of_kings_and_emperors%2C_Le_Petit_Journal_1898.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:China,_the_cake_of_kings_and_emperors,_Le_Petit_Journal_1898.jpg)

An 1898 depiction of Western powers [carving up China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_concessions_in_China "Foreign concessions in China").

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/Victor_Gillam_A_Thing_Well_Begun_Is_Half_Done_1899_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1136_01.jpg/145px-Victor_Gillam_A_Thing_Well_Begun_Is_Half_Done_1899_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1136_01.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Victor_Gillam_A_Thing_Well_Begun_Is_Half_Done_1899_Cornell_CUL_PJM_1136_01.jpg)

A depiction of America building [connections to the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panama_Canal "Panama Canal") and its Filipino colony (left) after the 1898 [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War "Spanish–American War").

[Western imperialism in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_imperialism_in_Asia "Western imperialism in Asia") from the 18th to 20th centuries coincided with the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution") in the West and the dethroning of India and China as the world's foremost economies.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-61) The [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire "British Empire") first became dominant in South Asia, with most of the region being [conquered by British traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Company_rule_in_India "Company rule in India") in the late 18th and early 19th centuries before falling under [direct British rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Raj "British Raj") after a failed [1857 revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1857_revolt "1857 revolt"); the 1869 completion of the [Suez Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Canal "Suez Canal"), which increased British access to India, went on to [further European influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Imperialism "New Imperialism") over Africa and Asia.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62) Around this time, Western powers started to dominate China in what later became known as the [century of humiliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Century_of_humiliation "Century of humiliation"), with the British-supported [opium trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_opium_in_China#Growth_of_the_opium_trade "History of opium in China") and later [Opium Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opium_Wars "Opium Wars") resulting in China being forced into an unprecedented situation of importing more than it exported.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-63)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-64)

Foreign domination of China was furthered by the [Japanese colonial empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_colonial_empire "Japanese colonial empire"), which controlled some of East Asia and briefly much of Southeast Asia (which had earlier been [taken over](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonisation_of_Southeast_Asia "European colonisation of Southeast Asia") by the British, Dutch and French in the late 19th century),[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-65) [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea "New Guinea") and the [Pacific islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_islands "Pacific islands"); Japan's domination was enabled by its rapid rise that had taken place during the [Meiji era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_era "Meiji era") of the late 19th century, in which it applied industrial knowledge learned from the West and thus overtook the rest of Asia.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-66)[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67) One significant influence on Japan had been the United States, which had begun projecting influence across the Pacific after its early-to-mid-19th century [westward expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westward_expansion "Westward expansion").[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68) The [breakup of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decline_of_the_Ottoman_Empire "Decline of the Ottoman Empire") in the early 20th century led to the Middle East also being [contested and partitioned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Middle_East#Modern_Middle_East "History of the Middle East") by the British and French.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-69)

### Contemporary era

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Contemporary era")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg/220px-Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Soviet_Union_and_China_map_including_the_three_co-bordering_countries.svg)

The Soviet Union (in red) and China (yellow) controlled most of Asia in the late 20th century

With the end of [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") in 1945 and the wartime ruination of Europe and imperial Japan, many countries in Asia were able to rapidly [free themselves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decolonisation_of_Asia "Decolonisation of Asia") of colonial rule.[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70) The [independence of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_of_India "Independence of India") came along with the [carving out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_of_India "Partition of India") of a separate nation for the majority of [South Asian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_Muslims "South Asian Muslims"), which in 1971 [further split](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh_Liberation_War "Bangladesh Liberation War") into Pakistan and [Bangladesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangladesh "Bangladesh");[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71) The [Cold War in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War_in_Asia "Cold War in Asia") strained relations between India and Pakistan and affected Asia more generally.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72) The [end of the Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_the_Cold_War "End of the Cold War") and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union "Dissolution of the Soviet Union") by 1991 saw the independence of the five modern Central Asian countries.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-73)

Some Arab countries took economic advantage of massive oil deposits that were discovered in their territory, becoming globally influential,[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74) though stability in the Middle East has been affected since 1948 by the [Arab–Israeli conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab%E2%80%93Israeli_conflict "Arab–Israeli conflict") and American-led [interventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_foreign_policy_in_the_Middle_East "United States foreign policy in the Middle East").[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75)[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-76) East Asian nations (along with Singapore in Southeast Asia) became economically prosperous with high-growth "[tiger economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiger_economies "Tiger economies")";[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) China, having undergone [market-driven reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_economic_reform "Chinese economic reform") under [Deng Xiaoping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping "Deng Xiaoping"),[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78) regained its place among the top two economies of the world by the 21st century.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) India has also grown significantly because of [economic liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_liberalisation_in_India "Economic liberalisation in India") that started in the 1990s,[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80) with extreme poverty now below 20%;[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81) India and China's rise has coincided with growing tensions between the two, with the [Indo-Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pacific "Indo-Pacific") now an actively contested area between China and counterbalancing forces.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-82)[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-83)

## Geography

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Geography")]

Main article: [Geography of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Asia "Geography of Asia")

See also: [Category:Biota of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Biota_of_Asia "Category:Biota of Asia")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/79/Himalayas.jpg/220px-Himalayas.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Himalayas.jpg)

The [Himalayan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himalayas "Himalayas") range is home to some of the planet's highest peaks

Asia is the largest continent on Earth. It covers 9% of the Earth's total surface area (or 30% of its land area), and has the longest coastline, at 62,800 kilometres (39,022 mi). Asia is generally defined as comprising the eastern four-fifths of Eurasia. It is located to the east of the [Suez Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Canal "Suez Canal") and the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains "Ural Mountains"), and south of the [Caucasus Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus_Mountains "Caucasus Mountains") (or the [Kuma–Manych Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuma%E2%80%93Manych_Depression "Kuma–Manych Depression")) and the [Caspian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_Sea "Caspian Sea") and [Black Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Sea "Black Sea").[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-National_Geographic-1999-15)[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85) It is bounded on the east by the [Pacific Ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Ocean "Pacific Ocean"), on the south by the Indian Ocean and on the north by the Arctic Ocean. Asia is subdivided into 49 countries, five of them ([Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_\(country\) "Georgia (country)"), [Azerbaijan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijan "Azerbaijan"), [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia "Russia"), [Kazakhstan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazakhstan "Kazakhstan") and [Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkey "Turkey")) are [transcontinental countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcontinental_countries "Transcontinental countries") lying partly in Europe. Geographically, Russia is partly in Asia, but is considered a [European nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Europe "List of sovereign states and dependent territories in Europe"), both [culturally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_culture "Russian culture") and politically.

The [Gobi Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gobi_Desert "Gobi Desert") is in Mongolia and the [Arabian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Desert "Arabian Desert") stretches across much of the Middle East. The [Yangtze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yangtze "Yangtze") in China is the longest river in the continent. The Himalayas between Nepal and China is the tallest mountain range in the world. [Tropical rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest "Tropical rainforest") stretch across much of southern Asia and coniferous and [deciduous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deciduous "Deciduous") forests lie farther north.

### Main regions

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Main regions")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png/310px-Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Detailed_map_of_Asian_regions.png)

Detailed map of Asian regions

There are various approaches to the regional division of Asia. The following subdivision into regions is used, among others, by the [United Nations Statistics Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Statistics_Division "United Nations Statistics Division") (UNSD). This division of Asia into regions by the United Nations is done solely for statistical reasons and does not imply any assumption about political or other affiliations of countries and territories.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)

- [North Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Asia "North Asia") ([Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberia "Siberia"))[\[note 3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-87)
- [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia")
- [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia "West Asia") (The [Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East "Middle East") or [Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_East "Near East") and part of the [Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caucasus "Caucasus"))
- [South Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia "South Asia") ([Indian subcontinent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_subcontinent "Indian subcontinent"))
- [East Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia "East Asia") ([Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_East "Far East"))
- [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia") ([East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Indies "East Indies") and [Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indochina "Indochina"))

### Climate

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Climate")]

Main article: [Climate of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Asia "Climate of Asia")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/Koppen-Geiger_Map_Asia_present.svg/310px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_Asia_present.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_Asia_present.svg)

[Köppen-Geiger climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") map for Asia[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-88)

Asia has extremely diverse climate features. Climates range from [Arctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic "Arctic") and [subarctic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subarctic "Subarctic") in Siberia to tropical in southern India and Southeast Asia. It is moist across southeast sections, and dry across much of the interior. Some of the largest daily temperature ranges on Earth occur in western sections of Asia. The [monsoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsoon "Monsoon") circulation dominates across southern and eastern sections, due to the presence of the Himalayas forcing the formation of a thermal low which draws in moisture during the summer. Southwestern sections of the continent are hot. Siberia is one of the coldest places in the [Northern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Hemisphere "Northern Hemisphere"), and can act as a source of arctic air masses for North America. The most active place on Earth for [tropical cyclone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_cyclone "Tropical cyclone") activity lies northeast of the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines") and south of Japan.

#### Climate change

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Climate change")]

This section is an excerpt from [Climate change in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_in_Asia "Climate change in Asia").\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Climate_change_in_Asia\&action=edit)]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg/440px-2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27%2C_2021_vs._August_27%2C_2022_in_Sindh.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2022_Pakistan_Floods_-_August_27,_2021_vs._August_27,_2022_in_Sindh.jpg)

The [2022 South Asian floods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_South_Asian_floods "2022 South Asian floods"), including in [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Pakistan_floods "2022 Pakistan floods") (pictured) are an example of a climate change impact.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89)[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-90)

[Climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change") is particularly important in Asia, as the continent accounts for the majority of the human population. Warming since the 20th century is increasing the threat of [heatwaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heatwave "Heatwave") across the entire continent.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  Heatwaves lead to increased mortality, and the demand for [air conditioning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning "Air conditioning") is rapidly accelerating as the result. By 2080, around 1 billion people in the cities of [South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asia "South Asia") and [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia") are expected to experience around a month of extreme heat every year.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1460  The [impacts on water cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_the_water_cycle "Effects of climate change on the water cycle") are more complicated: already arid regions, primarily located in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia "West Asia") and [Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asia "Central Asia"), will see more [droughts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drought "Drought"), while areas of [East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asia "East Asia"), Southeast and South Asia which are already wet due to the [monsoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsoon "Monsoon") will experience more flooding.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459 

The waters around Asia are subjected to the same [impacts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_oceans "Effects of climate change on oceans") as elsewhere, such as the increased warming and [ocean acidification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_acidification "Ocean acidification").[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1465  There are many [coral reefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coral_reef "Coral reef") in the region, and they are highly vulnerable to climate change,[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  to the point practically all of them will be lost if the warming exceeds 1.5 °C (2.7 °F).[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_ArmstrongMcKay2022-92)[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_ArmstrongMcKayExplainer-93) Asia's distinctive [mangrove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove "Mangrove") ecosystems are also highly vulnerable to [sea level rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise "Sea level rise").[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  Asia also has more countries with large coastal populations than any other continent, which would cause large economic impacts from sea level rise.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  Water supplies in the [Hindu Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Kush "Hindu Kush") region will become more unstable as its enormous [glaciers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier "Glacier"), known as the "Asian water towers", gradually melt.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  These changes to water cycle also affect vector-borne disease distribution, with malaria and dengue fever expected to become more prominent in the tropical and subtropical regions.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1459  [Food security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_security "Food security") will become more uneven, and South Asian countries could experience significant impacts from global food price volatility.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1494 

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6c/Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png/220px-Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Kang_2018_NCP_irrigation_RCPs.png)

Climate change is expected to exacerbate [heat stress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_stress "Heat stress") over at the [North China Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_China_Plain "North China Plain"), which is particularly vulnerable as widespread [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation "Irrigation") results in very moist air. There is a risk that agricultural labourers will be physically unable to work outdoors on hot summer days at the end of the century, particularly under the scenario of greatest emissions and warming.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_Kang2018-94)

Historical emissions from Asia are lower than those from Europe and North America. However, China has been the single largest emitter of greenhouse gases in the 21st century, while India is the third-largest. As a whole, Asia currently accounts for 36% of world's primary [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy "Energy") consumption, which is expected to increase to 48% by 2050. By 2040, it is also expected to account for 80% of the world's [coal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal "Coal") and 26% of the world's [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_gas "Natural gas") consumption.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1468  While the [United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States "United States") remains the world's largest [oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil "Oil") consumer, by 2050 it is projected to move to third place, behind China and India.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1470  While nearly half of the world's new [renewable energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renewable_energy "Renewable energy") capacity is built in Asia,[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1470  this is not yet sufficient in order to meet the goals of the [Paris Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Agreement "Paris Agreement"). They imply that the renewables would account for 35% of total energy consumption in Asia by 2030.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1471 

[Climate change adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation "Climate change adaptation") is already a reality for many Asian countries, with a wide range of strategies attempted across the continent.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1534  Important examples include the growing implementation of [climate-smart agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate-smart_agriculture "Climate-smart agriculture") in certain countries or the "[sponge city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sponge_city "Sponge city")" planning principles in China.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1534  While some countries have drawn up extensive frameworks such as the Bangladesh Delta Plan or Japan's Climate Adaptation Act,[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1508  others still rely on localized actions that are not effectively scaled up.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Climate_change_in_Asia_AR6_WGII_Chapter10-91): 1534 

## Economy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Economy")]

Main articles: [Economy of Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Asia "Economy of Asia"), [List of Asian countries by GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_GDP "List of Asian countries by GDP"), [List of countries in Asia-Pacific by GDP (nominal)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_in_Asia-Pacific_by_GDP_\(nominal\) "List of countries in Asia-Pacific by GDP (nominal)"), and [List of Asian and Pacific countries by GDP (PPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Asian_and_Pacific_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\) "List of Asian and Pacific countries by GDP (PPP)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg/260px-1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1_Singapore_city_skyline_2010_day_panorama.jpg)

Singapore has one of the [busiest container ports in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_container_ports "List of busiest container ports") and is the world's fourth-largest [foreign exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_exchange "Foreign exchange") trading hub

Asia has the [largest continental economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_continents_by_GDP "List of continents by GDP") in the world by both [GDP nominal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\) "List of countries by GDP (nominal)") and [PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity "Purchasing power parity") values, and is the fastest growing economic region.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-95) As of 2023[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit), [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China "China") is by far the largest economy on the continent, making up nearly half of the continent's economy by GDP nominal. It is followed by Japan, India, South Korea, Indonesia, Saudi Arabia and Turkey, which are all ranked among the top 20 largest economies both by nominal and PPP values.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) Based on Global Office Locations 2011, Asia dominated the office locations with 4 of the top 5 being in Asia: Hong Kong, Singapore, [Tokyo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo "Tokyo") and [Seoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seoul "Seoul"). Around 68% of international firms have an office in Hong Kong.[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97)

In the late 1990s and early 2000s, the economy of China[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98) had an average annual growth rate of more than 8%. According to economic historian [Angus Maddison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angus_Maddison "Angus Maddison"), India had the world's largest economy for much of the past three millennia prior to the 19th century, accounting for 25% of the world's industrial output.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99)[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101)[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-102) China was the [largest and most advanced economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_China "Economic history of China") on earth for much of recorded history and shared the mantle with India.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Eric.ed.gov-2000-17)[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104) For several decades in the late twentieth century Japan was the largest economy in Asia and second-largest of any single nation in the world, after surpassing the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") (measured in net material product) in 1990 and Germany in 1968. (NB: A number of supernational economies are larger, such as the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union") (EU), the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement "North American Free Trade Agreement") (NAFTA) or [APEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/APEC "APEC")). This ended in 2010 when China overtook Japan to become the world's second largest economy. It is forecasted that India will overtake Japan in terms of nominal GDP by 2027.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-95)

In the late 1980s and early 1990s, Japan's GDP by currency exchange rates was almost as large as that of the rest of Asia combined.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-95) In 1995, Japan's economy nearly equaled that of the US as the largest economy in the world for a day, after the Japanese currency reached a record high of 79 [yen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yen "Yen")/US$. Economic growth in Asia since [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") to the 1990s had been concentrated in Japan as well as the four regions of South Korea, Taiwan, Hong Kong and Singapore located in the [Pacific Rim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Rim "Pacific Rim"), known as the [Four Asian Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Asian_Tigers "Four Asian Tigers"), which are now all considered developed economies, having among the highest GDP per capita in Asia.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-International_Monetary_Fund-95)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg/220px-Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mumbai_skyline_BWSL.jpg)

Mumbai is one of the most populous cities in Asia, as well as an economic and tourism hub

Asia is the largest continent in the world by a considerable margin, and it is rich in natural resources, such as [petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleum "Petroleum"), [forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest "Forest"), [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish "Fish"), [water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water "Water"), [rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rice "Rice"), [copper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copper "Copper") and [silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver "Silver"). Manufacturing in Asia has traditionally been strongest in East and Southeast Asia, particularly in China, [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan"), South Korea, Japan, India, the Philippines, and Singapore. Japan and South Korea continue to dominate in the area of [multinational corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_corporation "Multinational corporation"), but increasingly the PRC and India are making significant inroads. Many companies from Europe, North America, South Korea and Japan have operations in Asia's developing countries to take advantage of its abundant supply of cheap labour and relatively developed infrastructure.[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-106)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)

According to [Citigroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citigroup "Citigroup") in 2011, 9 of 11 [Global Growth Generators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3G_countries "3G countries") countries came from Asia driven by population and income growth. They are Bangladesh, China, India, Indonesia, Iraq, Mongolia, the Philippines, Sri Lanka and Vietnam.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-108) Asia has three main financial centers: Hong Kong, Tokyo and Singapore. [Call centers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Call_center "Call center") and [business process outsourcing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Business_process_outsourcing "Business process outsourcing") (BPOs) are becoming major employers in India and the Philippines due to the availability of a large pool of highly skilled, English-speaking workers. The increased use of outsourcing has assisted the rise of India and the China as financial centers. Due to its large and extremely competitive information technology industry, India has become a major hub for outsourcing.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-109)

Trade between Asian countries and countries on other continents is largely carried out on the sea routes that are important for Asia. Individual main routes have emerged from this. The main route leads from the Chinese coast south via Hanoi to Jakarta, Singapore and Kuala Lumpur through the [Strait of Malacca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Malacca "Strait of Malacca") via the Sri Lankan Colombo to the southern tip of India via Malé to East Africa [Mombasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mombasa "Mombasa") (see also: [Indo-Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Pacific "Indo-Pacific")), from there to [Djibouti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djibouti "Djibouti"), then through the Red Sea over the [Suez Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suez_Canal "Suez Canal") into Mediterranean (see also: [Indo-Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Mediterranean "Indo-Mediterranean")), there via Haifa, Istanbul and [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens "Athens") to the upper Adriatic to the northern Italian hub of [Trieste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trieste "Trieste") with its rail connections to Central and Eastern Europe or further to [Barcelona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barcelona "Barcelona") and around Spain and France to the European northern ports. A far smaller part of the goods traffic runs via [South Africa to Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_Route "Cape Route"). A particularly significant part of the Asian goods traffic is carried out on the [Pacific Rim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Rim "Pacific Rim"), toward [Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles "Los Angeles") and [Long Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Beach "Long Beach"). The melting of the Arctic is also paving the way for [new shipping routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_shipping_routes "Arctic shipping routes") from Northeast Asia to Europe and North America.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-110) The land route to Europe are the subject of construction projects, comparatively smaller in scope. smaller in terms of scope. Intra-Asian trade, including sea trade, is growing rapidly.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-111)[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112)[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113)[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-114)[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115)[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-116)[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-117)

In 2010, Asia had 3.3 million millionaires (people with net worth over US$1 million excluding their homes), slightly below North America with 3.4 million millionaires. In 2011, Asia topped Europe in number of millionaires.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-118) Citigroup in The Wealth Report 2012 stated that the total wealth of people in Asia with over $100 million in assets exceeded that of their North American counterparts for the first time, as the world's "economic center of gravity" continued moving east. At the end of 2011, there were 18,000 Asian people mainly in Southeast Asia, China and Japan who have at least $100 million in disposable assets, while North America with 17,000 people and Western Europe with 14,000 people.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-119)

## Tourism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Asia\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Tourism")]

[![A Thai temple complex with several ornate buildings, and a lot of visitors](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/08/%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg/220px-%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%95%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%94%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A1-5.jpg)

[Wat Phra Kaew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wat_Phra_Kaew "Wat Phra Kaew") in the [Grand Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Palace "Grand Palace") is among [Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok "Bangkok")'s major tourist attractions.

See also the categories [Tourism in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Tourism_in_Asia "Category:Tourism in Asia") and [Transport in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Transport_in_Asia "Category:Transport in Asia")

With growing Regional Tourism with domination of Chinese visitors, [MasterCard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MasterCard "MasterCard") has released Global Destination Cities Index 2013 with 10 of 20 are dominated by Asia and Pacific Region Cities and also for the first time a city of a country from Asia ([Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok "Bangkok")) set in the top-ranked with 15.98 million international visitors.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124)
